<!--
Hi everyone! I’m Josh – I’m a full time independent open source maintainer. I focus on static analysis tooling around JavaScript & TypeScript – most notably, typescript-eslint, the tooling that allows you to run ESLint and Prettier on TypeScript code.
I’m also the author of the Learning TypeScript book, published by O’Reilly.
-->

<!--
In this talk, I'd like to walk you through a feature of TypeScript called "generics". We'll start by covering the foundations of TypeScript types, introduce generic types and functions, cover a host of practical features and fun stuff you can do with them, then end with some cautionary notes about how to use them well.
-->

<!--
JavaScript is a programming language. It provides a standard set of syntax to describe values and how to use those values: variables, string literals, assignments, and so on.
The big problem with JavaScript is that it doesn’t have a built-in, standardized way to describe the intent behind those values. This code snippet shows creating a value variable, then assigning it first a string, then a number. There’s no way in JavaScript to say, hey, I want this value to hold a string.
-->

---

transition: { name: fade, duration: 700 }
---

<!--
The TypeScript programming language looks a lot like JavScript.
TypeScript adds in some new syntax to the world of JavaScript. Here we see a ‘type annotation’ indicating that our value variable should only ever be a string.
-->

transition: { name: fade, duration: 700 }

JavaScript has a lot of features in it and can take some time to learn. What is it about types that so many people think they're worth it to add to the list of things you need to know for JavaScript?
-->

<div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:104.38px;left:0px;top:0px;width:960px;justify-content:center;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:49px;font-weight:700;line-height:52.92px;text-align:center"><span style="color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:49px;font-weight:700">🧐 What Are Types? 🧐</span></p></div>

<!--
Being able to describe those shapes in a standardized, enforceable syntax is an incredibly useful tool for development. Let's look at it in practice.
-->

---

layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 700 }

-->

</div>

<!--
If we assign 'boxed' an object whose value property is a string, then great! We're doing what we told TypeScript we would. Everyone's happy.
If we assign 'boxed' an object whose value property isn't a string, though, we get a type error. TypeScript is rightfully telling us that our declared intent (string) doesn't match what we provided (number). Thanks, TypeScript!
On the other hand, what if we want another variable to be a Boxed with value: number? It would be nice if we could make generalized types, where only some parts can be swapped out as needed…
-->

---

layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 700 }
---

</div>

<!--
Introducing, generic types! Generic types allow us to declare reusable shapes in the type system.
-->

---

layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 700 }
---

<div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:118.12px;left:65.5px;top:270px;width:828px;justify-content:center;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:35px;font-weight:500;line-height:37.8px;margin-top:10px;text-align:center"><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:35px;font-weight:500">How do we make types generically reusable?</span></p></div>
</div>

<!--
We're going to now spend some time defining generics and how they can be used for generalized, reusable types.
-->

---

layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 700 }

<div class="pptx" style="">

<div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:104.38px;left:0px;top:0px;width:960px;justify-content:center;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:49px;font-weight:700;line-height:52.92px;text-align:center"><span style="color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:49px;font-weight:700">🧐 What Are Generic Types? 🧐</span></p></div>

</div>

<!--
Take this new Boxed interface. Instead of always declaring its 'value' property to be a string, it takes a 'type parameter' called 'T'.
Just to be clear on naming here, Boxed is a generic interface. T is a type parameter. The existence of a type parameter is what makes the interface generic.
Later own, consumers of this interface can provide a type argument for that T. We could provide a string to make a Boxed string like before, or other types if we want.
-->

<div class="pptx" style="">
<img class="pptx-shape" src="/media/image11.png" alt="Screenshot of the opener to https://www.totaltypescript.com/no-such-thing-as-a-generic.  There Is No Such Thing As A Generic Matt Pocock Matt Pocock Matt is a well-regarded TypeScript expert known for his ability to demystify complex TypeScript concepts. If you don't understand generics in TypeScript, I think there's something you've misunderstood.  There is no such thing as a 'generic'.  There are generic types, generic functions, and generic classes.  There are type arguments and type parameters.  You cannot 'pass' a generic, 'declare' it, or 'infer' it.  In other words, 'generic' is not a noun, it's an adjective." style="height:540px;left:179.75px;top:0px;width:600.5px">
</div>

<!--
I'm particular about phrasing because it's very common for developers to call type parameters 'generics'. Phrases like "passing a generic" are ambiguous and misleading, and I've seen a lot of people struggle with learning generics because they Google or ChatGPT the wrong terms and get confused.
I highly recommend this wonderful Total TypeScript article from Matt Pocock, There Is No Such Thing As A Generic.
https://www.totaltypescript.com/no-such-thing-as-a-generic
-->

-->

<div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:104.38px;left:0px;top:0px;width:960px;justify-content:center;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:49px;font-weight:700;line-height:52.92px;text-align:center"><span style="color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:49px;font-weight:700">🧐 What Are Generic Types? 🧐</span></p></div>
</div>

<!--
Another way of thinking about generic types is that they're like functions in the type system. You provide some input, and they give you an output. In this case, types are the inputs, and you get an output type.
-->

---

layout: none
transition: { name: view-transition, duration: 700 }
---

<!--
The identity function is supposed to return the same type as whatever you pass it. For example, if you provide a string, the return type should be string. How do we describe this in TypeScript types?
-->

---

layout: none
transition: { name: view-transition, duration: 700 }

<!--
If we pass in a number argument instead of a string, we'd want the return type to be number, not string. If we hardcode value's type to be string, TypeScript gives a type error instead.
-->

<!--
They declare a relationship: depending on what you pass in, the type of what you get back out is different.
I think that's a really nifty way of thinking of things. It takes a bit to wrap your head around, but it's the right mental model for a lot of the wacky type operations we'll see later.
Let's mix things up and start going into those type system operations.
-->

<!--
Suppose you wanted to create a piece of state similar to how a lot of UI libraries these days work. You might declare a createState function that takes in an initial value and returns an object with properties to get or set that value. Well, this is a great use case for generics, since the types of get and set are functions that use the type of initialValue.
Rewording from earlier, the type of the provided initialValue changes the type of the object returned by createState.
-->

<!--
I would write this function's types something like this: a generic State <T> interface with two properties. 'get' is a function that returns T. 'set' is a function that takes in a T and returns the void type, meaning we ignore the result (it doesn't return anything we care about).
The createState function also has a type parameter, which is used for its initialValue type and for its explicit return type, State <T>. Fun!
-->

<!--
Now, before we go into deeper features around generics, I want to talk a bit about our createBox function's inference. TypeScript inferred the 'string' type when we provided 'abc'. But it actually didn't have to.
-->

---

transition: { name: fade, duration: 700 }
---

<div class="pptx" style="">

<!--
In TypeScript-land, you can use types more specific than the general string primitive. You can alternately use what's called 'literal' types: specific instances of those types.
-->

transition: { name: view-transition, duration: 700 }
---

<!--
We can tell it to go with the most constant, narrow, specific type possible in inference with a 'const' modifier on the type parameter. This is called a 'const type parameter' and it's a relatively newer feature of TypeScript.
When used on createBox, we can see that it changed the return type from Box<string> to Box<"abc">.
Now, you might be wondering - why is this useful? Well, TypeScript is able to give you better assistance and documentation during development when your types are more specific.
-->

<!--
I would also add a type parameter 'K' for the 'key' parameter... but then we get a TypeScript type error that we can't index 'T' using 'K'. Which is actually true - we never specified that 'K' is one of the keys of 'T'!
With this type as-is, someone could provide a totally unrelated key, like 'asdf' or the number 123, and nothing in our types said to stop them.
-->

---

layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 700 }
---

<div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:28.48px;left:605.67px;top:149.53px;width:115.45px;justify-content:flex-end;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'Menlo';font-size:17px;line-height:20.4px"><span style="color:#CCCCCC;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:17px">: </span><span style="color:#569CD6;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:17px">keyof </span><span style="color:#4EC9B0;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:17px">T</span></p></div>
<div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:29.08px;left:408.26px;top:149.93px;width:33.63px;justify-content:flex-end;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'Menlo';font-size:17px;line-height:20.4px"><span style="color:#CCCCCC;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:17px">&gt;</span></p></div>
</div>

<!--
The common strategy in TypeScript for restricting a value to the keys of a type is with the 'keyof' operator. This function says that 'key' must be one of the keys of the 'T' type parameter.
...and it kind of works! No type errors.
...but the return type isn't exactly what we wanted. It's string | boolean, the union. We wanted just boolean.
The imprecise return type is happening because the 'key' parameter isn't generic. Nothing in the types says that the function's return type should change based on what we provide for key.
We need some way to give 'key' a type parameter, but also only allow it to be a keyof T. In other words, we need to 'constrain' key's type parameter.
-->

---

layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 700 }
---

<div class="pptx" style="">
<div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:119.15px;left:0px;top:236.68px;width:960px;justify-content:flex-end;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:42px;font-weight:500;line-height:50.4px;margin-top:10px;text-align:center"><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:42px;font-weight:500">Use </span><span style="color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:35px">extends</span><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:42px;font-weight:500"> to constrain a type parameter</span></p><p style="font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:42px;font-weight:500;line-height:50.4px;margin-top:10px;text-align:center"><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:42px;font-weight:500">to a type, such as another type parameter.</span></p></div>
<div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:104.38px;left:0px;top:0px;width:960px;justify-content:center;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:49px;font-weight:700;line-height:52.92px;text-align:center"><span style="color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:49px;font-weight:700">Type Constraints</span></p></div>
</div>

<!--
Which we can do! TypeScript allows these type parameter 'constraints' with the 'extends' keywords. It's the equivalent of saying "make a type parameter, and it must be assignable to some other type" - like a type annotation for a type parameter.
-->

---

layout: none
transition: { name: view-transition, duration: 700 }
---

<div class="pptx" style="">
<div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:31.5px;left:44.03px;top:486.38px;width:872.73px;justify-content:flex-end;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:20px;font-weight:700;line-height:24px"><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:20px;font-weight:700">) &#123;</span></p></div>
<div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" <img class="pptx-shape" src="/media/image19.png" alt="Screenshot of the top few paragraphs of https://effectivetypescript.com/2020/08/12/generics-golden-rule.  Effective TypeScript The Golden Rule of Generics Wed 12 August 2020  The New TypeScript Handbook has some real gems in it. Here's what it has to say about generics:  Writing generic functions is fun, and it can be easy to get carried away with type parameters. Having too many type parameters or using constraints where they aren't needed can make inference less successful, frustrating callers of your function.  It goes on to offer a few specific pieces of advice about how to use generics, including one that I've started to think of as the &quot;Golden Rule of Generics&quot;:  Type Parameters Should Appear Twice Type parameters are for relating the types of multiple values. If a type parameter is only used once in the function signature, it's not relating anything.  Rule: If a type parameter only appears in one location, strongly reconsider if you actually need it." style="height:537.38px;left:131px;top:1.31px;width:698px">
<div v-click="1" class="pptx-group"><div class="pptx-shape pptx-crop" style="height:143.38px;left:131px;top:394px;width:698px"><img src="/media/image19.png" alt="Cropped screenshot a note in https://effectivetypescript.com/2020/08/12/generics-golden-rule.  Type Parameters Should Appear Twice Type parameters are for relating the types of multiple values. If a type parameter is only used once in the function signature, it's not relating anything.  Rule: If a type parameter only appears in one location, strongly reconsider if you actually need it." style="height:537.38px;left:0px;position:absolute;top:-394px;width:698px"></div></div>

<!--
I know there’s a lot of stuff in the JavaScript ecosystem that can take up your time. Linting is one of many things to learn and set up. Plus, when you have larger projects, getting ESLint and typescript-eslint hooked up right can be a real chore - especially in older versions. But I promise you, if you have the bandwidth to try these things out, it is almost certainly worth it to use at least the recommended and type-checked rules from typescript-eslint.
-->

<!--
The last piece of advice I’ll give is something I affectionately call the TypeScript Type Cycle. It’s a variant of the Gartner Hype Cycle, which describes the three phases of inflated excitement, disillusionment, and eventual productivity new areas of technology go through. I think every TypeScript developer who learns generics eventually gets through this type cycle.
The first phase is the peak of inflated expectations. Upon getting a handle on generics, TypeScript developers feel great about themselves -- as they should! They mastered the type system; all sorts of wild and wacky features like const type parameters and constraints and whatnot are at their disposal! TypeScript!! Yay!!!
But, those wild and wacky types come at a cost. They’re harder to write, harder to read, and much harder to debug. Turns out many of the generics they used at the peak were unnecessary and over-inflated code complexity. Thus we exit the Peak of Inflated Types and enter the Trough of Type Disillusionment.
Fear not: we all learn from our mistakes. That’s what it means to be a developer. Having learned the hard way not to overuse TypeScript’s type system features, developers enter the third phase in their TypeScript journey: the Plateau of Productivity. It’s here that TypeScript shines the best.
-->

<div class="pptx" style="">
<img class="pptx-shape" src="/media/image11.png" alt="Screenshot of the opener to https://www.totaltypescript.com/no-such-thing-as-a-generic.  There Is No Such Thing As A Generic Matt Pocock Matt Pocock Matt is a well-regarded TypeScript expert known for his ability to demystify complex TypeScript concepts. If you don't understand generics in TypeScript, I think there's something you've misunderstood.  There is no such thing as a 'generic'.  There are generic types, generic functions, and generic classes.  There are type arguments and type parameters.  You cannot 'pass' a generic, 'declare' it, or 'infer' it.  In other words, 'generic' is not a noun, it's an adjective." style="height:540px;left:179.75px;top:0px;width:600.5px">
</div>

<img class="pptx-shape" src="/media/image19.png" alt="Screenshot of the top few paragraphs of https://effectivetypescript.com/2020/08/12/generics-golden-rule.  Effective TypeScript The Golden Rule of Generics Wed 12 August 2020  The New TypeScript Handbook has some real gems in it. Here's what it has to say about generics:  Writing generic functions is fun, and it can be easy to get carried away with type parameters. Having too many type parameters or using constraints where they aren't needed can make inference less successful, frustrating callers of your function.  It goes on to offer a few specific pieces of advice about how to use generics, including one that I've started to think of as the &quot;Golden Rule of Generics&quot;:  Type Parameters Should Appear Twice Type parameters are for relating the types of multiple values. If a type parameter is only used once in the function signature, it's not relating anything.  Rule: If a type parameter only appears in one location, strongly reconsider if you actually need it." style="height:537.38px;left:131px;top:1.31px;width:698px">
